<p><b>Sir Robert Gordon Menzies</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Thistle" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Thistle">KT</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of Australia">AK</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of the Companions of Honour">CH</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Counsel" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Counsel">QC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Australian_Academy_of_Science" class="mw-redirect" title="Fellow of the Australian Academy of Science">FAA</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (20 December 1894 – 15 May 1978) was an Australian politician who was the 12th and longest-serving <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, holding office from 1939 to 1941 and again from 1949 to 1966. He played a central role in the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>, defining its policies and its broad outreach.
</p><p>Menzies studied law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">University of Melbourne</a> and became one of <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a>'s leading lawyers. He was <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Premier_of_Victoria" title="Deputy Premier of Victoria">Deputy Premier of Victoria</a> from 1932 to 1934, and then transferred to <a href="/wiki/Federal_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal Parliament">Federal Parliament</a>, subsequently becoming <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Attorney-General of Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Industry_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Industry (Australia)">Minister for Industry</a> in the government of <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Lyons" title="Joseph Lyons">Joseph Lyons</a>. In April 1939, following Lyons's death, Menzies <a href="/wiki/1939_United_Australia_Party_leadership_election" title="1939 United Australia Party leadership election">was elected</a> leader of the <a href="/wiki/United_Australia_Party" title="United Australia Party">United Australia Party</a> (UAP) and sworn in as Prime Minister. He authorised <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_World_War_II" title="Military history of Australia during World War II">Australia's entry into World War II</a> in September 1939, and spent four months in England to participate in meetings of <a href="/wiki/Churchill_war_ministry" title="Churchill war ministry">Churchill's war cabinet</a>. On his return to Australia in August 1941, Menzies found that he had lost the support of his party and consequently resigned as Prime Minister. He subsequently helped to create the new Liberal Party, and was elected its inaugural leader in August 1945.
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1949_Australian_federal_election" title="1949 Australian federal election">1949 federal election</a>, Menzies led the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal–Country coalition</a> to victory and returned as prime minister. His appeal to the home and family, promoted via reassuring radio talks, matched the national zeitgeist as the economy grew and middle-class values prevailed; the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a>'s support had also been eroded by <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> scares. After 1955, <a href="/wiki/Menzies_government_(1949%E2%80%9366)" class="mw-redirect" title="Menzies government (1949–66)">his government</a> also received support from the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Labour_Party_(Australia)" title="Democratic Labour Party (Australia)">Democratic Labour Party</a>, a breakaway group from the Labor Party. Menzies won seven consecutive elections during his second term, eventually retiring as prime minister in January 1966. Despite the failures of his first administration, his government is remembered for its development of Australia's capital city of <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>, its expanded <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Australia" title="Immigration to Australia">post-war immigration scheme</a>, emphasis on higher education, and national security policies, which saw Australia contribute troops to the <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Korean_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Military history of Australia during the Korean War">Korean War</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Malayan_Emergency" title="Military history of Australia during the Malayan Emergency">Malayan Emergency</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Indonesia%E2%80%93Malaysia_confrontation" title="Military history of Australia during the Indonesia–Malaysia confrontation">Indonesia–Malaysia confrontation</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_Vietnam_War" title="Military history of Australia during the Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Birth and family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Robert Gordon Menzies was born on 20 December 1894 at his parents' home in <a href="/wiki/Jeparit,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeparit, Victoria">Jeparit, Victoria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)5_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)5-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He was the fourth of five children born to Kate (<i>née</i> Sampson) and <a href="/wiki/James_Menzies_(Australian_politician)" title="James Menzies (Australian politician)">James Menzies</a>; he had two elder brothers, an elder sister, and a younger brother. Menzies was the first Australian prime minister to have two Australian-born parents: his father was born in <a href="/wiki/Ballarat" title="Ballarat">Ballarat</a> and his mother in <a href="/wiki/Creswick,_Victoria" title="Creswick, Victoria">Creswick</a>. His grandparents on both sides had been drawn to Australia by the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_gold_rush" title="Victorian gold rush">Victorian gold rush</a>. His maternal grandparents were born in <a href="/wiki/Penzance" title="Penzance">Penzance</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cornwall" title="Cornwall">Cornwall</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> His paternal grandfather, also named Robert Menzies, was born in <a href="/wiki/Renfrewshire_(historic)" title="Renfrewshire (historic)">Renfrewshire</a>, Scotland, and arrived in Melbourne in 1854.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)1_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The following year he married Elizabeth Band, the daughter of a cobbler from <a href="/wiki/Fife" title="Fife">Fife</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)2_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)2-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Menzies was proud of his Scottish heritage, and preferred his surname to be pronounced in the traditional Scottish manner (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ŋ/: &#39;ng&#39; in &#39;sing&#39;">ŋ</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">MING</span>-iss</i></a>) rather than as it is spelled (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ɛ/: &#39;e&#39; in &#39;dress&#39;">ɛ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="&#39;z&#39; in &#39;zoom&#39;">z</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span><span title="&#39;z&#39; in &#39;zoom&#39;">z</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">MEN</span>-zeez</i></a>). This gave rise to his nickname "Ming", which was later expanded to "<a href="/wiki/Ming_the_Merciless" title="Ming the Merciless">Ming the Merciless</a>" after the comic strip character.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His middle name was given in honour of <a href="/wiki/Charles_George_Gordon" title="Charles George Gordon">Charles George Gordon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-haz14_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-haz14-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Menzies family had moved to Jeparit, a small <a href="/wiki/Wimmera" class="mw-redirect" title="Wimmera">Wimmera</a> township, in the year before Robert's birth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> At the 1891 census, the settlement had a population of just 55 people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)7_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)7-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> His elder siblings had been born in Ballarat, where his father was a locomotive painter at the <a href="/wiki/Phoenix_Foundry" title="Phoenix Foundry">Phoenix Foundry</a>. Seeking a new start, he moved the family to Jeparit to take over the general store,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> which "survived rather than prospered".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)7_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)7-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> During Menzies's childhood, three of his close relatives were elected to parliament. His uncle <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Menzies" title="Hugh Menzies">Hugh</a> was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Assembly" title="Victorian Legislative Assembly">Victorian Legislative Assembly</a> in 1902, followed by his father in 1911, while another uncle, <a href="/wiki/Sydney_Sampson" title="Sydney Sampson">Sydney Sampson</a>, was elected to the federal <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> in 1906.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Each of the three represented rural constituencies, and were defeated after a few terms. Menzies's maternal grandfather John Sampson was active in the trade union movement. He was the inaugural president of the Creswick Miners' Association, which he co-founded with future <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> MP <a href="/wiki/William_Spence" title="William Spence">William Spence</a>, and was later prominent in the Amalgamated Miners' Association.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)4-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Childhood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Menzies,_age_13.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg/220px-Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg/330px-Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg/440px-Robert_Menzies%2C_age_13.jpg 2x" data-file-width="966" data-file-height="668" /></a><figcaption>Article in <i><a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Punch" title="Melbourne Punch">Melbourne Punch</a></i> detailing Menzies's feat of topping the state school examinations at the age of 13</figcaption></figure>
<p>Growing up, Menzies and his siblings "had the normal enjoyments and camaraderies of a small country town".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)11_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)11-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He began his formal education in 1899 at the Jeparit State School, a single-teacher <a href="/wiki/One-room_school" title="One-room school">one-room school</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-haz14_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-haz14-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> When he was about eleven, he and his sister were sent to Ballarat to live with his paternal grandmother; his two older brothers were already living there. In 1906, Menzies began attending the Humffray Street State School in <a href="/wiki/Bakery_Hill,_Victoria" title="Bakery Hill, Victoria">Bakery Hill</a>. The following year, aged 13, he ranked first in the state-wide scholarship examinations. This feat financed the entirety of his secondary education, which had to be undertaken at private schools, as Victoria did not yet have a system of public secondary schools.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)12_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)12-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1908 and 1909, Menzies attended <a href="/wiki/Grenville_College,_Ballarat" title="Grenville College, Ballarat">Grenville College</a>, a small private school in <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Central" title="Ballarat Central">Ballarat Central</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)15_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)15-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He and his family moved to <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a> in 1910, where he enrolled in <a href="/wiki/Wesley_College,_Melbourne" class="mw-redirect" title="Wesley College, Melbourne">Wesley College</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)18_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)18-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Menzies was "not very interested in and certainly incompetent at sport", but excelled academically. In his third and final year at Wesley he won a £40 exhibition for university study, one of 25 awarded by the state government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)19_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)19-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="University">University</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: University">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1913, Menzies entered the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Law_School" title="Melbourne Law School">Melbourne Law School</a>. He won a variety of prizes, exhibitions, and scholarships during his time as a student, graduating as a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">Bachelor of Laws</a> (LL.B.) in 1916 and a <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Laws" title="Master of Laws">Master of Laws</a> (LL.M.) in 1918. He did, however, fail Latin in his first year.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> One of his prize-winning essays, <i>The Rule of Law During the War</i>, was published as a brochure with an introduction by <a href="/wiki/Harrison_Moore" title="Harrison Moore">Harrison Moore</a>, the law school dean. In 1916, Menzies was elected president of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne_Student_Union" title="University of Melbourne Student Union">Student Representatives' Council</a> and editor of the <i>Melbourne University Magazine</i>. He wrote both prose and poetry for the magazine,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)20_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)20-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> and also contributed a song about "little <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>" to an end-of-year <a href="/wiki/Revue" title="Revue">revue</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)24_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)24-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Menzies was also president of the Students' Christian Union, a founding member of the Historical Society, and a prominent member of the Law Students' Society. He had "a reputation as an "unusually bright and articulate member of the undergraduate community", and was known as a skilful debater.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)20_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)20-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> However, he had also begun to develop the traits of pomposity and arrogance that would cause difficulties later in his career. His fellow law student and future parliamentary colleague <a href="/wiki/Percy_Joske" title="Percy Joske">Percy Joske</a> noted Menzies as a student "did not suffer fools gladly [...] the trouble was that his opponents frequently were not fools and that he tended to say things that were not only cutting and unkind but that were unjustified".<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During World War I, Menzies served his compulsory militia service in the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_University_Regiment" title="Melbourne University Regiment">Melbourne University Rifles</a> (a part-time militia unit)<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> from 1915 to 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> This unit was not efficient and Menzies noted in his diary that training in even basic skills such as rifle shooting was sub-standard.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He was commissioned a second lieutenant on 6 January 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Unlike many of his contemporaries, he did not volunteer for overseas service, something that would later be used against him by political opponents; in 1939 he described it as "a stream of mud through which I have waded at every campaign in which I have participated". Menzies never publicly addressed the reasons for his decision not to enlist, stating only that they were "compelling" and related to his "intimate personal and family affairs".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)275_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)275-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> His two older brothers did serve overseas. In a 1972 interview, his brother <a href="/wiki/Frank_Menzies" title="Frank Menzies">Frank Menzies</a> recalled that a "family conference" had determined that Robert should not enlist. They believed that having two of the family's three adult sons serving overseas was a sufficiently patriotic contribution to the war effort, and that the family's interests would be served best by Robert continuing his academic career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)30_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)30-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Another reason for keeping one of the elder sons home was the health of their father, James, who was physically unwell and emotionally unstable at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> It has been noted that, as a student, Menzies supported the introduction of <a href="/wiki/1916_Australian_conscription_referendum" title="1916 Australian conscription referendum">compulsory overseas conscription</a>, which if implemented would have made him one of the first to be conscripted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)29-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Promoted to lieutenant, he resigned his commission with effect from 16 February 1921.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legal_career">Legal career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Legal career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After graduating from the University of Melbourne in 1916 with first-class honours in Law, Menzies was admitted to the Victorian Bar and to the High Court of Australia in 1918. Establishing his own practice in Melbourne, Menzies specialised chiefly in constitutional law which he had read with the leading Victorian jurist and future High Court judge, Sir <a href="/wiki/Owen_Dixon" title="Owen Dixon">Owen Dixon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In 1920, Menzies was an advocate for the Amalgamated Society of Engineers which eventually <a href="/wiki/Amalgamated_Society_of_Engineers_v_Adelaide_Steamship_Co_Ltd" title="Amalgamated Society of Engineers v Adelaide Steamship Co Ltd">took its appeal to the High Court of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The case became a landmark authority for the positive reinterpretation of Commonwealth powers over those of the States. The High Court's verdict raised Menzies's profile as a skilled advocate, and eventually he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Counsel" title="King&#39;s Counsel">King's Counsel</a> in 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career_in_politics">Early career in politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Early career in politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="State_politics">State politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: State politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Menzies_1930s.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Robert_Menzies_1930s.png/220px-Robert_Menzies_1930s.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="298" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Robert_Menzies_1930s.png/330px-Robert_Menzies_1930s.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Robert_Menzies_1930s.png/440px-Robert_Menzies_1930s.png 2x" data-file-width="898" data-file-height="1218" /></a><figcaption>Menzies as Deputy Premier of Victoria</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1928, Menzies entered state parliament as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Council" title="Victorian Legislative Council">Victorian Legislative Council</a> from <a href="/wiki/East_Yarra_Province" title="East Yarra Province">East Yarra Province</a>, representing the <a href="/wiki/Nationalist_Party_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalist Party of Australia">Nationalist Party of Australia</a>. He stood for constitutional democracy, the rule of law, the sanctity of contracts and the jealous preservation of existing institutions. Suspicious of the Labor Party, Menzies stressed the superiority of free enterprise except for certain public utilities such as the railways.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)66–67_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)66–67-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> His candidacy was nearly defeated when a group of ex-servicemen attacked him in the press for not having enlisted, but he survived this crisis. Within weeks of his entry to parliament, he was made a minister without portfolio in a new minority Nationalist State government led by Premier William McPherson. The new government had formed when the previous Labor government lost the support of the cross-bench Country Progressives.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The following year he shifted to the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Assembly" title="Victorian Legislative Assembly">Victorian Legislative Assembly</a> as the member for the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Nunawading" title="Electoral district of Nunawading">Electoral district of Nunawading</a>. In 1929, he founded the Young Nationalists as his party's youth wing and was its first president. Holding the portfolios of Attorney-General and Minister for the Railways, Menzies was <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Premier_of_Victoria" title="Deputy Premier of Victoria">Deputy Premier of Victoria</a> from May 1932 until July 1934.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Federal_politics">Federal politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Federal politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In August 1934, Menzies resigned from state parliament to contest the federal <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Kooyong" title="Division of Kooyong">Division of Kooyong</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1934_Australian_federal_election" title="1934 Australian federal election">upcoming general election</a> for the <a href="/wiki/United_Australia_Party" title="United Australia Party">United Australia Party</a> (UAP), which was the result of a merger during his tenure as a state parliamentarian of the Nationalists, Labor dissidents and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Party" title="Australian Party">Australian Party</a>.
</p><p>The previous member, <a href="/wiki/John_Latham_(judge)" title="John Latham (judge)">John Latham</a>, had given up the seat in order to prepare for appointment as <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Australia" title="Chief Justice of Australia">Chief Justice of Australia</a>. Kooyong was a safely conservative seat based on <a href="/wiki/Kew,_Victoria" title="Kew, Victoria">Kew</a>, and Menzies won easily. He was immediately appointed <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Attorney-General of Australia</a> and Minister for Industry in the <a href="/wiki/Lyons_government" title="Lyons government">Lyons government</a>. In 1937 he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Counsellor</a>. In late 1934 and early 1935 Menzies, then attorney-general, unsuccessfully prosecuted the government's highly controversial case for the <a href="/wiki/Attempted_exclusion_of_Egon_Kisch_from_Australia" title="Attempted exclusion of Egon Kisch from Australia">attempted exclusion</a> from Australia of <a href="/wiki/Egon_Kisch" title="Egon Kisch">Egon Kisch</a>, a Czech Jewish communist.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> The initial prohibition on Kisch's entry to Australia, however, had not been imposed by Menzies but by the Country Party minister for the interior, Thomas Paterson.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Menzies had extended discussions with British experts on Germany in 1935, but could not make up his mind whether <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a> was a "real German patriot" or a "mad swash-buckler". He expressed both views with an inclination to the former, says historian David Bird. In published essays in 1936, he called for a "live and let-live" attitude.<sup id="cite_ref-Bird2014_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bird2014-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He condemned Nazi antisemitism, in 1933 writing to the organisers of an anti-Nazi protest at the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Town_Hall" title="Melbourne Town Hall">Melbourne Town Hall</a> that "I hope that I may be associated with the protest of the meeting tonight against the barbaric and medieval persecution to which their fellow Jews in Europe are apparently being subjected".<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> As late as July 1939, Menzies, by then Prime Minister, declared in a speech that "history will label Hitler as one of the great men of the century".<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1938, while Attorney-General, Menzies spent several weeks on an official visit to <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a>. He was strongly committed to democracy for the British peoples, but he initially thought that the Germans should take care of their own affairs.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He strongly supported the appeasement policies of the Chamberlain government in London, and sincerely believed that war could and should be avoided at all costs.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> after the visit to Germany in 1938, Menzies wrote that the "abandonment by the Germans of individual liberty ... has something rather magnificent about it".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Menzies also praised the "really spiritual quality in the willingness of Germans to devote themselves to the service and well-being of the state".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Menzies supported British foreign policy, including appeasement, and was initially reticent about the prospect of going to war with Germany. However, by September 1939 the unfolding crisis in Europe changed his public stance that the diplomatic efforts by Chamberlain and other leaders to broker a peace agreement had failed, and that war was now an inevitability. In his Declaration of War broadcast on 3 September 1939, Menzies explained the dramatic turn of events over the past twelve months necessitating this change of course: </p><blockquote><p>In those past 12 months, what has happened? in cold-blooded breach of the solemn obligations implied in both the statements I have quoted, Hitler has annexed the whole of the <a href="/wiki/Czechoslovakia" title="Czechoslovakia">Czechoslovak state</a>. Has, without flickering an eyelid, made a pact with Russia, a country the denouncing and reviling of which has been his chief stock-in-trade ever since he became chancellor. And has now, under circumstances which I will describe to you, invaded with armed force and in defiance of civilised opinion, the independent nation of <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>. Your own comments on this dreadful history will need no reinforcement by me. All I need say is, that whatever the inflamed ambitions of the German Führer may be, he will undoubtedly learn, as other great enemies of freedom have learned before, that no empire, no dominion, can be soundly established upon a basis of broken promises or dishonoured agreements.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> </p></blockquote><p>Menzies went on to say that if Hitler's expansionist "policy were allowed to go unchecked there could be no security in Europe and there could be no just peace for the world".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> However, in a letter written by Menzies on 11 September 1939, he privately urged for peace negotiations and the continuation of appeasement with Hitler.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Meanwhile, on the domestic front, animosity developed between Sir Earle Page and Menzies which was aggravated when Page became Acting Prime Minister during Lyons's illness after October 1938. Menzies and Page attacked each other publicly. He later became deputy leader of the UAP. His supporters began promoting him as Lyons's natural successor; his critics accused Menzies of wanting to push Lyons out, a charge he denied.
</p><p>In 1938, as part of the <a href="/wiki/1938_Dalfram_dispute" title="1938 Dalfram dispute">Dalfram dispute</a>, he was ridiculed as <i>Pig Iron Bob</i>, the result of an industrial conflict with the <a href="/wiki/Waterside_Workers%27_Federation_of_Australia" title="Waterside Workers&#39; Federation of Australia">Waterside Workers' Federation of Australia</a> whose members had refused to load Australian <a href="/wiki/Pig_iron" title="Pig iron">pig iron</a> being sold to an arms manufacturer in the <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Empire of Japan</a>, for that <a href="/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War" title="Second Sino-Japanese War">country's war against China</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1939, he resigned from the Cabinet in protest at postponement of <a href="/wiki/National_Insurance_(Australia)" title="National Insurance (Australia)">the national insurance scheme</a> and insufficient expenditure on defence.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="First_prime_ministership">First prime ministership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: First prime ministership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Danzig_crisis">The Danzig crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: The Danzig crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg/220px-Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="295" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg/330px-Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg/440px-Nla.pic-an23217367-v.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1340" /></a><figcaption>Declaration of War Broadcast, September 1939</figcaption></figure>
<p>With Lyons's sudden death on 7 April 1939, Page became caretaker prime minister until the UAP could elect a new leader. On 18 April 1939, Menzies <a href="/wiki/1939_United_Australia_Party_leadership_election" title="1939 United Australia Party leadership election">was elected</a> party leader over three other candidates. He was sworn in as prime minister eight days later.
</p><p>A crisis arose almost immediately, however, when Page refused to serve under him. In an extraordinary personal attack in the House, Page accused Menzies of cowardice for not having enlisted in the War, and of treachery to Lyons. Menzies then formed a minority government. When Page was deposed as Country Party leader a few months later, Menzies took the Country Party back into his government in a full-fledged Coalition, with Page's successor, Archie Cameron, as number-two-man in the government. 
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/Danzig_crisis" title="Danzig crisis">Danzig crisis</a>, Menzies, who had long felt that the Treaty of Versailles was too harsh towards Germany, supported having the Free City of Danzig (modern <a href="/wiki/Gda%C5%84sk" title="Gdańsk">Gdańsk</a>) rejoin Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Through Hitler had only asked for extraterritorial highways across the Polish Corridor to link East Prussia with the rest of the <i>Reich</i>, Menzies also supported having the Polish Corridor returned to Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Despite his support for the Germans' claims to Danzig, Menzies was opposed to the prospect of German domination of Europe. Menzies in a speech on 28 March 1939 declared: "I do not accept this doctrine of an unimpeded march by Germany to a territorial conquest of middle and southeastern Europe".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970171_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970171-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, the fear of Japan proved decisive for Menzies, who believed that for Australia to not support Britain in the Danzig crisis would lead to Britain eventually abandoning Australia to face Japan alone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The prospect of war in Europe threatened to undermine the <a href="/wiki/Singapore_strategy" title="Singapore strategy">Singapore strategy</a> that called for the main British battle fleet to be sent out to Singapore (the major British naval base in Asia) in the event of a threat from Japan.<sup id="cite_ref-Murfett_2010_p._18_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Murfett_2010_p._18-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> A war with Germany would require the majority of the Royal Navy's ships to stay within European waters, and an inability to execute the Singapore strategy as planned was widely feared in Canberra to be a strong encouragement for Japan to strike south against Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-Murfett_2010_p._18_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Murfett_2010_p._18-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> However, Menzies had a strong faith in the ability of <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> to handle the Danzig crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172_41-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Menzies believed that the crisis should and would be resolved by a Munich-type deal under which the Free City of Danzig would be peacefully allowed to "go home to the <i>Reich</i>" while at the same time Germany should and would be deterred from going to war against Poland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172_41-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970172-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Though Menzies felt that Poland was in the wrong in opposing the return of the Free City to Germany, he did not want to see the crisis end with Poland being subjected to Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several times, Menzies declared that there were "two sides" both equally worthy of consideration to the Danzig dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He dismissed the "jitters", as he called it, on the stock market in the summer of 1939 while he spoke of "an outpouring of the sentiments of peace in the minds of the people".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>  Throughout the summer of 1939, Menzies stressed in his speeches that war was "not inevitable" as he urged Australians to enjoy their summer vacations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178_44-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>  In the cabinet, Menzies was opposed to expanding the tiny Australian Army beyond its current size of 1, 571 soldiers on economic grounds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178_44-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970178-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The news of the Molotov–Ribbentrop pact of 22 August 1939 was greeted with both relief and concern in Menzies's cabinet. It was understood that the German-Soviet nonaggression pact ended the prospect of a "peace front" of Britain, France and the Soviet Union to deter Germany from invading Poland, and that war was more likely than not in Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Conversely, the Molotov–Ribbentrop pact had (temporarily as it proved) alienated Japan from Germany, and within the Australian cabinet, it was felt that Japan would be less likely to enter a war against the British empire because of the German-Soviet agreement, which had come as a most unwanted surprise in Tokyo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In Tokyo, the failure to achieve an alliance with Germany alongside the shock of the German-Soviet non-aggression pact caused the Japanese Prime Minister Baron <a href="/wiki/Hiranuma_Kiichir%C5%8D" title="Hiranuma Kiichirō">Hiranuma Kiichirō</a>  to submit his resignation to the Emperor, which was accepted. The possibility that at least for the moment that Japan would stay neutral if the Danzig crisis led to war in Europe encouraged the cabinet in Canberra to stand by Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews1970179-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="At_war">At war</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: At war">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 3 September 1939 Britain and France declared war on Germany due to its invasion of Poland on 1 September, leading to the start of World War II.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Menzies responded immediately by also declaring Australia to be at war in support of Britain, and delivered a radio broadcast to the nation on that same day, which began "Fellow Australians. It is my melancholy duty to inform you officially that in consequence of a persistence by Germany in her invasion of Poland, Great Britain has declared war upon her and that, as a result, Australia is also at war."<sup id="cite_ref-:2_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> A couple of days after the declaration, Menzies recalled parliament and asked for general support as the government faced the enormous responsibility of leading the nation in war time. Page and Curtin, as party leaders, pledged their support for all that needed to be done for the defence of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)285_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)285-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thus, Menzies at the age of 44 found himself a wartime leader of a small nation of 7&#160;million people. He was especially worried about the military threat from Japan. Australia had very small forces, and depended on Britain for defence against the looming threat of the Japanese Empire, with its 100&#160;million people, a very powerful Army and Navy and an aggressive foreign policy. He hoped that a policy of appeasement would head off a war with Japan, and repeatedly pressured London.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Menzies did his best to rally the country, but the bitter memories of the disillusionment which followed <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> made his task difficult; this was compounded by his lack of a service record. Furthermore, as attorney-general and deputy prime minister, he had made an official visit to Germany in 1938, when the official policy of the Australian government, supported by the Opposition, was strong support for <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a>'s policy of <a href="/wiki/Appeasement" title="Appeasement">Appeasement</a>. Menzies, then also holding the responsibility for the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Munitions" title="Department of Munitions">Department of Munitions</a> created a couple of months earlier, led the Coalition into the <a href="/wiki/1940_Australian_federal_election" title="1940 Australian federal election">1940 election</a> and suffered an eight-seat swing, losing the slender majority he had inherited from Lyons. The result was a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a>, with the Coalition two seats short of a majority. Menzies managed to form a minority government with the support of two independent MPs, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Coles" title="Arthur Coles">Arthur Coles</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Wilson_(Australian_politician)" title="Alexander Wilson (Australian politician)">Alex Wilson</a>. Labor, led by <a href="/wiki/John_Curtin" title="John Curtin">John Curtin</a>, refused Menzies's offer to form a war coalition, and opposed using the Australian army for a European war, preferring to keep it at home to defend Australia. Labor agreed to participate in the <a href="/wiki/Advisory_War_Council_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Advisory War Council (Australia)">Advisory War Council</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Strategic_debates">Strategic debates</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Strategic debates">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Menzies sent the bulk of the army to help the British in the Middle East and Singapore, and told <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> the Royal Navy needed to strengthen its Far Eastern forces.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> On 11 August 1940, Churchill sent a long letter to Menzies promising that if Japan entered the war, Britain would activate the Singapore strategy by sending a strong Royal Navy force to Singapore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>  Menzies continued to be worried about the fact that the Singapore base-despite being billed as the "Gibraltar of the East" was a base, not the fortress that it was presented as; about the shoddy state of the fortifications at Singapore; and about the lack of details in Churchill's promises.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>  
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg/220px-Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="295" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg/330px-Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg/440px-Menzies_Churchill_WW21941.jpg 2x" data-file-width="893" data-file-height="1197" /></a><figcaption>Australian Prime Minister, Robert Menzies, with his British counterpart, Winston Churchill, London 1941</figcaption></figure>
<p>Just before the September 1940 election, on 13 August 1940, <a href="/wiki/1940_Canberra_air_disaster#Casualties" title="1940 Canberra air disaster">three members of Menzies's cabinet</a> had been killed in an air crash—the <a href="/wiki/1940_Canberra_air_disaster" title="1940 Canberra air disaster">Canberra air disaster</a>—along with General <a href="/wiki/Brudenell_White" title="Brudenell White">Brudenell White</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chief_of_Army_(Australia)" title="Chief of Army (Australia)">Chief of the General Staff</a>; two other passengers and four crew.<sup id="cite_ref-can-times-disaster_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-can-times-disaster-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> This event weakened Menzies's government.<sup id="cite_ref-tink-destroy-gov_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tink-destroy-gov-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> On the night of 11 November 1940, aircraft from the carriers of the Royal Navy's Mediterranean fleet disabled a number of Italian capital ships at anchor at the Italian base at Taranto. In December 1940, Menzies sent Churchill a letter expressing concern that the Imperial Japanese Navy might likewise use air power to cripple the Singapore base.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In the same letter, Menzies asked Churchill to activate the Singapore strategy by stationing at least three or four capital ships at Singapore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In response, Churchill wrote back to Menzies that to say he would not have capital ships "sitting idle" at Singapore and that to transfer capital ships to Singapore would mean "ruining the Mediterranean situation. This I am sure you would not want to do unless or until the Japanese danger became far more menacing than at present".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In March 1941 and again in August 1941, Menzies appealed to Churchill in letters to activate the Singapore strategy as he wrote that he was highly concerned about Japanese ambitions in the Asia-Pacific region.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBell201473_49-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBell201473-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 24 January 1941, Menzies spent four months in Britain discussing war strategy with Churchill and other Empire leaders, while his position at home deteriorated. <i>En route</i> to the UK he took the opportunity to stop over to visit Australian troops serving in the <a href="/wiki/North_African_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="North African Campaign">North African Campaign</a>. On 20 February 1941, Churchill asked Menzies to give his approval to sent the Australian forces in North Africa to Greece.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Like many other Australians of his generation, Menzies was haunted by the memory of the <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli_campaign" title="Gallipoli campaign">Battle of Gallipoli</a>-which came about because of Churchill's plans to sent an Allied fleet up the Dardanelles-and he was highly suspicious of another of Churchill's plans for victory in the Mediterranean.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> On 25 February 1941, Menzies  reluctantly gave his approval to sent the <a href="/wiki/6th_Division_(Australia)" title="6th Division (Australia)">6th Division</a> to Greece.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558_52-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStockingsHancock2013558-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Professor <a href="/wiki/David_Day_(historian)" title="David Day (historian)">David Day</a>, an Australian historian, has posited that Menzies might have replaced Churchill as British prime minister, and that he had some support in the UK for this. Support came from <a href="/wiki/Viscount_Astor" title="Viscount Astor">Viscount Astor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lord_Beaverbrook" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Beaverbrook">Lord Beaverbrook</a> and former WWI Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> who were trenchant critics of Churchill's purportedly autocratic style, and favoured replacing him with Menzies, who had some public support for staying on in the <a href="/wiki/War_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="War Cabinet">War Cabinet</a> for the duration, which was strongly backed by Sir <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Hankey" class="mw-redirect" title="Maurice Hankey">Maurice Hankey</a>, former WWI Colonel and member of both the WWI and WWII War Cabinets. Writer <a href="/wiki/Gerard_Henderson" title="Gerard Henderson">Gerard Henderson</a> has rejected this theory, but history professors <a href="/wiki/Judith_Brett" title="Judith Brett">Judith Brett</a> and <a href="/wiki/Joan_Beaumont" title="Joan Beaumont">Joan Beaumont</a> support Day, as does Menzies's daughter, Heather Henderson, who claimed Lady Astor "even offered all her sapphires if he would stay on in England".<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Menzies came home to a hero's welcome. However, his support in Parliament was less certain. Not only did some Coalition MPs doubt his popularity in the electorate, but they also believed that a national unity government was the only long-term solution. Menzies's reputation was badly damaged by the failure of the Allied expedition to Greece, in which Australian troops played a prominent role.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHill201056_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHill201056-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian_Worker" title="The Australian Worker">The Australian Worker</a></i> newspaper in an editorial on 11 June 1941 attacked Menzies for having "too readily acquiesced in the ill-starred Greek campaign, of which the Cretan misadventure was the inevitable sequel...As a military adventure, it was madness. As a political gesture, it was stupid because it was doomed to failure."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHill201056_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHill201056-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Menzies sought to blame General Sir <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Blamey" title="Thomas Blamey">Thomas Blamey</a> for the Greek expedition as he maintained that he had only given permission to sent the Australians to Greece after Blamey did not oppose it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHill201056_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHill201056-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Menzies maintained that if Blamey had given his disapproval in his opinion as a professional soldier, he would never sent the Australians to Greece.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHill201056_54-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHill201056-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Matters came to a head in August, when the cabinet voted to have Menzies return to London to speak for Australia's interests in the War Cabinet. However, since Labor and the Coalition were level (both sides had 36 MPs), Menzies needed the support of the Labor Party in order to travel to Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Amid rumours that Menzies's real intention was to launch a political career in Britain, Labor insisted that the crisis was too dire for Menzies to leave the country.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Downfall">Downfall</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Downfall">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>With Labor unwilling to support him travelling to London and his position within his own party room weakening, Menzies called an emergency cabinet meeting. He announced his intention to resign and advise the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hore-Ruthven,_1st_Earl_of_Gowrie" title="Alexander Hore-Ruthven, 1st Earl of Gowrie">Lord Gowrie</a> to commission Curtin as Prime Minister. The Cabinet instead urged Menzies to make another overture to Labor for a national unity government, but Labor turned the offer down. With his position now untenable, Menzies resigned the prime ministership on 27 August 1941.<sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A joint UAP-Country Party conference chose Country Party leader <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Fadden" title="Arthur Fadden">Arthur Fadden</a> as Coalition leader—and hence Prime Minister—even though the Country Party was the junior partner in the Coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Menzies was bitter about this treatment from his colleagues, and nearly left politics,<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> but was persuaded to become Minister for Defence Co-ordination in Fadden's cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-phb_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-phb-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Fadden_government" title="Fadden government">Fadden government</a> lasted only 40 days before being defeated on a confidence motion. On 9 October 1941, Menzies resigned as leader of the UAP after failing to convince his colleagues that he should become <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a> in preference to Fadden. He <a href="/wiki/1941_United_Australia_Party_leadership_election" title="1941 United Australia Party leadership election">was replaced</a> as UAP leader by former prime minister <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>, who was 79 years old at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Interregnum">Interregnum</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Interregnum">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Menzies.27s_Forgotten_People"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Menzies's_Forgotten_People">Menzies's <i>Forgotten People</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Menzies&#039;s Forgotten People">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During his time in the political wilderness Menzies built up a large popular base of support by his frequent appeals, often by radio, to ordinary non-elite working citizens whom he called 'the Forgotten People'—especially those who were not suburban and rich or members of organised labour. From November 1941, he began a series of weekly radio broadcasts reaching audiences across New South Wales, Victoria and Queensland. A selection of these talks was edited into a book bearing the title of his most famous address, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Forgotten_People" title="The Forgotten People">The Forgotten People</a></i>, delivered on 22 May 1942. In this landmark address, Menzies appealed to his support base:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I do not believe that the real life of this nation is to be found either in great luxury hotels and the petty gossip of so-called fashionable suburbs, or in the officialdom of the organised masses. It is to be found in the homes of people who are nameless and unadvertised, and who, whatever their individual religious conviction or dogma, see in their children their greatest contribution to the immortality of their race. The home is the foundation of sanity and sobriety; it is the indispensable condition of continuity; its health determines the health of society as a whole.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Menzies himself described <i>The Forgotten People</i> collection as 'a summarised political philosophy'. Representing the blueprint of his liberal philosophy, <i>The Forgotten People</i> encompassed a wide range of topics including Roosevelt's Four Freedoms, the control of the war, the role of women in war and peace, the future of capitalism, the nature of democracy and especially the role of the middle class, 'the forgotten people' of the title and their importance to Australia's future as a democracy.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> The addresses frequently emphasised the values which Menzies regarded as critical to shaping Australia's wartime and postwar policies. These were essentially the principles of liberalism: individual freedom, personal and community responsibility, the rule of law, parliamentary government, economic prosperity and progress based on private enterprise and reward for effort.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After losing the UAP leadership, Menzies moved to the backbench. Besides his overall sense of duty, the war would have made it nearly impossible for him to return to his legal practice in any event.<sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_55-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Labor won a crushing victory at the <a href="/wiki/1943_Australian_federal_election" title="1943 Australian federal election">1943 election</a>, taking 49 of 74 seats and 58.2 percent of the <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party-preferred vote</a> as well as a Senate majority. The Coalition, which had sunk into near-paralysis in opposition, was knocked down to only 19 seats. Hughes resigned as UAP leader, and Menzies <a href="/wiki/1943_United_Australia_Party_leadership_election" title="1943 United Australia Party leadership election">was elected</a> as his successor on the second ballot, defeating three other candidates. The UAP also voted to end the joint opposition arrangement with the Country Party, allowing Menzies to replace Fadden as opposition leader.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Formation_of_the_Liberal_Party">Formation of the Liberal Party</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Formation of the Liberal Party">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Soon after his return, Menzies concluded that the UAP was at the end of its useful life. Menzies called a conference of anti-Labor parties with meetings in Canberra on 13 October 1944 and again in Albury (NSW) in December 1944. At the Canberra conference, the fourteen parties, with the UAP as the nucleus, decided to merge as one new non-Labor party—the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>. The organisational structure and constitutional framework of the new party was formulated at the Albury Conference. Officially launched at the Sydney Town Hall on 31 August 1945, the Menzies-led Liberal Party of Australia inherited the UAP's role as senior partner in the Coalition. Curtin died in office in 1945 and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley" title="Ben Chifley">Ben Chifley</a>.
</p><p>The reconfigured Coalition faced its first national test in the <a href="/wiki/1946_Australian_federal_election" title="1946 Australian federal election">1946 election</a>. It won 26 of 74 seats on 45.9 percent of the two-party vote, and remained in minority in the Senate. Despite winning a seven-seat swing, the Coalition failed to make a serious dent in Labor's large majority.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1949_election_campaign">1949 election campaign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: 1949 election campaign">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Over the next few years, however, the anti-communist atmosphere of the early <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> began to erode Labor's support. In 1947, Chifley announced that he intended to nationalise Australia's private banks, arousing intense middle-class opposition which Menzies successfully exploited. In addition to campaigning against Chifley's bank nationalization proposal, Menzies successfully led the 'No' case for a referendum by the Chifley government in 1948 to extend commonwealth wartime powers to control rents and prices.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In the election campaign of 1949, Menzies and his party were resolved to stamp out the communist movement and to fight in the interests of free enterprise against what they termed as Labor's 'socialistic measures'. If Menzies won office, he pledged to counter inflation, extend child endowment and end petrol rationing.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> With the lower house enlarged from 74 to 121 seats, the Menzies Liberal/Country Coalition won the 1949 election with 74 House seats and 51.0 percent of the two-party vote but remained in minority in the Senate. Whatever else Menzies's victory represented, his anti-communism and advocacy for free enterprise had captured a new and formidable support base in postwar Australian society.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_prime_ministership">Second prime ministership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Second prime ministership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Menzies_headshot.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Robert_Menzies_headshot.jpg/220px-Robert_Menzies_headshot.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/01/Robert_Menzies_headshot.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="272" data-file-height="340" /></a><figcaption>Menzies in 1952</figcaption></figure> 
<p>After his election victory, Menzies returned to the office of Prime Minister on 19 December 1949.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cold_War_and_national_security">Cold War and national security</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Cold War and national security">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The spectre of communism and the threat it was deemed to pose to national security became the dominant preoccupation of the new government in its first phase. Menzies introduced legislation in 1950 to ban the Communist Party, hoping that the Senate would reject it and give him a trigger for a <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> election, but Labor let the bill pass. It was subsequently <a href="/wiki/Australian_Communist_Party_v_Commonwealth" title="Australian Communist Party v Commonwealth">ruled unconstitutional</a> by the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court</a>. But when the Senate rejected his banking bill, he <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_federal_election" title="1951 Australian federal election">called a double dissolution election</a>. At that election, the Coalition suffered a five-seat swing, winning 69 of 121 seats and 50.7 percent of the two-party vote. However, it won six seats in the Senate, giving it control of both chambers. Later in 1951 Menzies decided to hold a <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_Communist_Party_ban_referendum" title="1951 Australian Communist Party ban referendum">referendum</a> on the question of changing the Constitution to permit the parliament to make laws in respect of Communists and Communism where he said this was necessary for the security of the Commonwealth. If passed, this would have given a government the power to introduce a bill proposing to ban the Communist Party. Chifley died a few months after the 1951 election. The new Labor leader, <a href="/wiki/H._V._Evatt" title="H. V. Evatt">H. V. Evatt</a>, campaigned against the referendum on civil liberties grounds, and it was narrowly defeated. Menzies sent Australian troops to the <a href="/wiki/Korean_War" title="Korean War">Korean War</a>. Economic conditions deteriorated in the early 1950s and Labor was confident of winning the <a href="/wiki/1954_Australian_federal_election" title="1954 Australian federal election">1954 election</a>. Shortly before the election, Menzies announced that a Soviet diplomat in Australia <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Mikhaylovich_Petrov_(diplomat)" class="mw-redirect" title="Vladimir Mikhaylovich Petrov (diplomat)">Vladimir Petrov</a>, had defected, and that there was evidence of a Soviet spy ring in Australia, including members of Evatt's staff. Evatt felt compelled to state on the floor of Parliament that he'd personally written to Soviet Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Vyacheslav_Molotov" title="Vyacheslav Molotov">Vyacheslav Molotov</a>, who assured him there were no Soviet spy rings in Australia, bringing the House into silence momentarily before both sides of Parliament laughed at Evatt's naivety.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> scare was claimed by some to enable the Menzies government to win the election. The Menzies government won 64 of 121 seats and 49.3 percent of the two-party vote. Evatt accused Menzies of arranging Petrov's defection. The aftermath of the 1954 election caused a split in the Labor Party, with several anti-Communist members from <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (Australia)">Victoria</a> defecting to form the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_(Anti-Communist)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party (Anti-Communist)">Australian Labor Party (Anti-Communist)</a>. The new party directed its preferences to the Liberals, with the Menzies government re-elected with an increased majority at the <a href="/wiki/1955_Australian_federal_election" title="1955 Australian federal election">1955 election</a>. Menzies was re-elected almost as easily at the <a href="/wiki/1958_Australian_federal_election" title="1958 Australian federal election">1958 election</a>, again with the help of preferences from what had become the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Labor_Party_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Labor Party (historical)">Democratic Labor Party</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg/220px-Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="168" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg/330px-Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/Robert_Menzies_1960_colour.jpg 2x" data-file-width="428" data-file-height="326" /></a><figcaption>Menzies in 1960</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:HMQ_and_R_Menzies.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/HMQ_and_R_Menzies.jpg/220px-HMQ_and_R_Menzies.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/32/HMQ_and_R_Menzies.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="326" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Menzies hosting a royal visit of Queen Elizabeth II</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Menzies era saw immense regional changes, with post-war reconstruction and the withdrawal of European Powers and the British Empire from the Far East (including independence for India and Indonesia). In response to these geopolitical developments, the Menzies government maintained strong ties with Australia's traditional allies such as Britain and the United States while also reorienting Australia's foreign policy focus towards the Asia Pacific. 
With his first Minister for External Affairs, Percy Spender, the Menzies government signed the ANZUS treaty in San Francisco on 1 September 1951. Menzies later told parliament that this security pact between Australia, New Zealand and the United States was 'based on the utmost good will, the utmost good faith and unqualified friendship' saying 'each of us will stand by it'.<sup id="cite_ref-joshfrydenberg1_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-joshfrydenberg1-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> At the same time as strengthening the alliance with the United States, Menzies and Spender were committed to Australia being on 'good neighbour terms' with the countries of South and South East Asia. To help forge closer ties in the region, the Menzies government initiated the Colombo Plan that would see almost 40 000 students from the region come to study in Australia over the four subsequent decades.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Recognising the economic potential of a burgeoning postwar Japan, Menzies, together with Trade Minister Jack McEwan and his new minister for External Affairs, Richard Casey, negotiated the Commerce Agreement with Japan in 1957. This trade agreement was followed by bilateral agreements with Malaya in 1958 and Indonesia in 1959.<sup id="cite_ref-joshfrydenberg1_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-joshfrydenberg1-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MenziesAndGorton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/MenziesAndGorton.jpg/220px-MenziesAndGorton.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/MenziesAndGorton.jpg/330px-MenziesAndGorton.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0c/MenziesAndGorton.jpg 2x" data-file-width="424" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Menzies with Minister for the Navy <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a> in 1961</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Economic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Holt_and_Menzies.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Holt_and_Menzies.jpg/220px-Holt_and_Menzies.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Holt_and_Menzies.jpg/330px-Holt_and_Menzies.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Holt_and_Menzies.jpg/440px-Holt_and_Menzies.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1358" /></a><figcaption>Menzies with Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Throughout his second period in office, Menzies practised classical liberal economics with an emphasis on private enterprise and self-sufficiency in contrast to Labor's 'socialist objective'. Accordingly, the economic policy emphasis of the Menzies government moved towards tax incentives to release productive capacity, boosting export markets, research and undertaking public works to provide power, water and communications.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
