</p><p>In 1951, the top marginal tax rate for incomes above £10,000 (equivalent to $425,000 today<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (June 2022)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>) was 75 per cent under Menzies; from 1955 until the mid-1980s, the top marginal tax rate was 67 per cent.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_reform">Social reform</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Social reform">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:RobertMenzies.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/RobertMenzies.jpg/220px-RobertMenzies.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="309" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/44/RobertMenzies.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="303" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Menzies in 1963, towards the end of his reign in office</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1949, Parliament legislated to ensure that all <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_Australians" title="Aboriginal Australians">Aboriginal</a> ex-servicemen should have the <a href="/wiki/Suffrage" title="Suffrage">right to vote</a>. In 1961 a Parliamentary Committee was established to investigate and report to the Parliament on Aboriginal voting rights and in 1962, Menzies's <i>Commonwealth Electoral Act</i> provided that all Indigenous Australians should have the right to enrol and vote at federal elections.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1960, the Menzies government introduced a new pharmaceutical benefits scheme, which expanded the range of prescribed medicines subsidised by the government. Other social welfare measures of the government included the extension of the Commonwealth Child Endowment scheme, the pensioner medical and free medicines service, the Aged Persons' Homes Assistance scheme, free provision of life-saving drugs; the provision of supplementary pensions to dependent pensioners paying rent; increased rates of pension, unemployment and sickness benefits, and rehabilitation allowances; and a substantial system of <a href="/wiki/Tax_incentives" class="mw-redirect" title="Tax incentives">tax incentives</a> and rewards.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> In 1961, the <i>Matrimonial Causes Act</i> introduced a uniform divorce law across Australia, provided funding for marriage counselling services and made allowances for a specified period of separation as sufficient grounds for a divorce.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to the decision by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Canberra_and_Goulburn" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Canberra and Goulburn">Catholic Diocese of Goulburn</a> in July 1962 to close its schools in protest at the lack of government assistance, the Menzies government announced a new package of state aid for independent and Catholic schools. Menzies promised five million pounds annually for the provision of buildings and equipment facilities for science teaching in secondary schools. Also promised were 10 000 scholarships to help students stay at school for the last two years with a further 2 500 scholarships for technical schools.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014320–321_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014320–321-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Despite the historically firm Catholic support base of the Labor Party, the Opposition under Calwell opposed state aid before eventually supporting it with the ascension of Gough Whitlam as Labor leader.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg/220px-Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg/330px-Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg/440px-Official_Portrait_of_Robert_Menzies_by_Ivor_Hele.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1859" data-file-height="2480" /></a><figcaption>Parliament House Portrait of Menzies by <a href="/wiki/Ivor_Hele" title="Ivor Hele">Ivor Henry Thomas Hele</a>, 1955</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1965, the Menzies government took the decision to end open discrimination against married women in the public service, by allowing them to become permanent public servants, and allowing female officers who were already permanent public servants to retain that status after marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014351_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014351-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration_policy">Immigration policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Immigration policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Menzies government maintained and indeed expanded the Chifley Labor government's postwar immigration scheme established by Immigration Minister, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Calwell" title="Arthur Calwell">Arthur Calwell</a> in 1947. Beginning in 1949, Immigration Minister Harold Holt decided to allow 800 non-European war refugees to remain in Australia, and Japanese war brides to be admitted to Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In 1950, External Affairs Minister Percy Spender instigated the Colombo Plan, under which students from Asian countries were admitted to study at Australian universities, then in 1957, non-Europeans with 15 years' residence in Australia were allowed to become citizens. In a watershed legal reform, a 1958 revision of the Migration Act introduced a simpler system for entry and abolished the "dictation test" which had permitted the exclusion of migrants on the basis of their ability to take down a dictation offered in any European language. Immigration Minister, <a href="/wiki/Alick_Downer" title="Alick Downer">Sir Alexander Downer</a>, announced that 'distinguished and highly qualified Asians' might immigrate. Restrictions continued to be relaxed through the 1960s in the lead up to the Holt government's watershed <i>Migration Act, 1966</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
This was despite a discussion with radio 2UE's Stewart Lamb in 1955, in which Menzies was a defender of the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a>. </p><blockquote><p>(Menzies) "I don't want to see reproduced in Australia the kind of problem they have in South Africa or in America or increasingly in Great Britain. I think it's been a very good policy and it's been of great value to us and most of the criticism of it that I've ever heard doesn't come from these oriental countries it comes from wandering Australians.
</p><p>(Lamb) "For these years of course in the past Sir Robert you have been described as a racist."
</p><p>(Menzies) "Have I?"
</p><p>(Lamb) "I have read this, yes."
</p><p>
(Menzies) "Well if I were not described as a racist I'd be the only public man who hasn't been."<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Higher_education_expansion">Higher education expansion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Higher education expansion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Menzies government extended Federal involvement in higher education and introduced the Commonwealth scholarship scheme in 1951, to cover fees and pay a generous means-tested allowance for promising students from lower socioeconomic groups.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In 1956, a committee headed by Sir Keith Murray was established to inquire into the financial plight of Australia's universities, and Menzies's pumped funds into the sector under conditions which preserved the autonomy of universities.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In its support for higher education, the Menzies government tripled Federal government funding and provided emergency grants, significant increases in academic salaries, extra funding for buildings, and the establishment of a permanent committee, from 1961, to oversee and make recommendations concerning higher education.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014246_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014246-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Development_of_Canberra">Development of Canberra</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Development of Canberra">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Menzies government developed the city of Canberra as the national capital. In 1957, the Menzies government established the National Capital Development Commission as independent statutory authority charged with overseeing the planning and development of Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014350_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014350-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> During Menzies' time in office, the great bulk of the federal public service moved from the state capitals to Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014350_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014350-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life">Later life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Later life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><span><video id="mwe_player_1" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/220px--ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="220" height="124" data-durationhint="322" data-mwtitle="ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons" resource="/wiki/File:ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv" type="video/ogg; codecs=&quot;theora, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file, 512 × 288 (1.09 Mbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="512" data-height="288" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.120p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Lowest bandwidth VP9 (120P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 120P" data-transcodekey="120p.vp9.webm" data-width="214" data-height="120" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.160p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth WebM (160P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 160P" data-transcodekey="160p.webm" data-width="284" data-height="160" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.180p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="320" data-height="180" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Small VP9 (240P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.vp9.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b1/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv/ABC_Petrov_Affair.ogv.240p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Small WebM (240P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.webm" data-width="426" data-height="240" /></video></span><figcaption>1970 <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">ABC</a> interview with Menzies and <a href="/wiki/Allan_Fraser_(Australian_politician)" title="Allan Fraser (Australian politician)">Allan Fraser</a>, discussing the <a href="/wiki/Petrov_Affair" title="Petrov Affair">Petrov Affair</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Menzies turned 71 in December 1965 and began telling others of his intention to retire in the new year. He informed cabinet of his decision on 19 January 1966 and resigned as leader of the Liberal Party the following day; Harold Holt was <a href="/wiki/1966_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="1966 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">elected unopposed as his successor</a>. Holt's swearing-in was delayed by the death of Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Shane_Paltridge" title="Shane Paltridge">Shane Paltridge</a> on 21 January. Menzies and Holt were pall-bearers at Paltridge's state funeral in <a href="/wiki/Perth" title="Perth">Perth</a> on 25 January, before returning to Canberra where Menzies formally concluded his term on 26 January.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Menzies's farewell press conference was the first political press conference telecast live in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> He resigned from Parliament on 17 February, ending 32 years in Parliament (most of them spent as either a cabinet minister or opposition frontbencher), a combined 25 years as leader of the non-Labor Coalition, and 38 years as an elected official. To date, Menzies is the last Australian prime minister to leave office on his or her own terms. He was succeeded as Liberal Party leader and prime minister by his former treasurer, <a href="/wiki/Harold_Holt" title="Harold Holt">Harold Holt</a>. He left office at the age of 71 years, 1 month and 6 days, making him the oldest person ever to be prime minister. Although the coalition remained in power for almost another seven years (until the <a href="/wiki/1972_Australian_federal_election" title="1972 Australian federal election">1972 Federal election</a>), it did so under four different prime ministers, largely due to his successor's death, only 22 months after taking office.
</p><p>On his retirement he became the thirteenth chancellor of the University of Melbourne and remained the head of the university from March 1967 until March 1972. Much earlier, in 1942, he had received the first honorary degree of Doctor of Laws of Melbourne University. His responsibility for the revival and growth of university life in Australia was widely acknowledged by the award of honorary degrees in the Universities of Queensland, Adelaide, Tasmania, New South Wales, and the Australian National University and by thirteen universities in Canada, the United States and Britain, including Oxford and Cambridge. Many learned institutions, including the <a href="/wiki/Fellowship_of_the_Royal_College_of_Surgeons" class="mw-redirect" title="Fellowship of the Royal College of Surgeons">Royal College of Surgeons</a> (Hon. FRCS) and the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australasian_College_of_Physicians" title="Royal Australasian College of Physicians">Royal Australasian College of Physicians</a> (Hon. FRACP), elected him to Honorary Fellowships, and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Academy_of_Science" title="Australian Academy of Science">Australian Academy of Science</a>, for which he supported its establishment in 1954, made him a fellow (FAAS) in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
On 7 October 1965, Menzies was installed as the ceremonial office of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Warden_of_the_Cinque_Ports" title="Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports">Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports</a> and Constable of <a href="/wiki/Dover_Castle" title="Dover Castle">Dover Castle</a> as appointed by the Queen, which included an official residence at <a href="/wiki/Walmer_Castle" title="Walmer Castle">Walmer Castle</a> during his annual visits to Britain. At the end of 1966 Menzies took up a scholar-in-residence position at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a>. He presented a series of lectures, published the following year as <i>Central Power in the Australian Commonwealth</i>. He later published two volumes of memoirs. In March 1967 he was elected Chancellor of Melbourne University, serving a five-year term.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
In 1971, Menzies suffered a severe stroke and was permanently paralysed on one side of his body for the remainder of his life. He suffered a second stroke in 1972. His official biographer, Lady McNicoll wrote after his death in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i> that Menzies was "splendid and sharp right up until the end" also that "each morning he underwent <a href="/wiki/Physiotherapy" class="mw-redirect" title="Physiotherapy">physiotherapy</a> and being helped to face the day." In March 1977, Menzies accepted his knighthood of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Order of Australia</a> (<a href="/wiki/List_of_Knights_and_Dames_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Knights and Dames of the Order of Australia">AK</a>) from Queen Elizabeth in a wheelchair in the Long Room of the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Cricket_Ground" title="Melbourne Cricket Ground">Melbourne Cricket Ground</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Centenary_Test" title="Centenary Test">Centenary Test</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 27 September 1920, Menzies married <a href="/wiki/Pattie_Menzies" title="Pattie Menzies">Pattie Leckie</a> at <a href="/wiki/Kew,_Victoria" title="Kew, Victoria">Kew</a> <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian Church">Presbyterian Church</a> in Melbourne. Pattie Leckie was the eldest daughter of <a href="/wiki/John_Leckie_(Australian_politician)" title="John Leckie (Australian politician)">John Leckie</a>, a Deakinite <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Liberal_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Liberal Party">Commonwealth Liberal</a> who was elected the member for the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Benambra" title="Electoral district of Benambra">Electoral district of Benambra</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Assembly" title="Victorian Legislative Assembly">Victorian Legislative Assembly</a> in 1913. Soon after their marriage, the Menzies bought the house in Howard Street, <a href="/wiki/Kew,_Victoria" title="Kew, Victoria">Kew</a>, which would become their family home for 25 years. They had three surviving children: Kenneth (1922–1993), Robert Jr (known by his middle name, Ian; 1923–1974)<sup id="cite_ref-menziescollection.esrc.unimelb.edu.au_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-menziescollection.esrc.unimelb.edu.au-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> and a daughter, Margery (known by her middle name, Heather; born 1928).<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Another child died at birth.
</p><p>Kenneth was born in <a href="/wiki/Hawthorn,_Victoria" title="Hawthorn, Victoria">Hawthorn</a> on 14 January 1922. He married Marjorie Cook on 16 September 1949,<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> and had six children; Alec, Lindsay, Robert III, Diana, Donald, and Geoffrey. He died in <a href="/wiki/Kooyong,_Victoria" title="Kooyong, Victoria">Kooyong</a> on 8 September 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Ian and Heather were both born in <a href="/wiki/Kew,_Victoria" title="Kew, Victoria">Kew</a>, on 12 October 1923 and 3 August 1928, respectively. Ian was afflicted with an undisclosed illness for most of his life. He never married, nor had children, and died in 1974 in <a href="/wiki/East_Melbourne,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="East Melbourne, Victoria">East Melbourne</a> at the age of 50.<sup id="cite_ref-menziescollection.esrc.unimelb.edu.au_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-menziescollection.esrc.unimelb.edu.au-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Heather married <a href="/wiki/Peter_Henderson_(Australian_public_servant)" title="Peter Henderson (Australian public servant)">Peter Henderson</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Diplomat" title="Diplomat">diplomat</a> and <a href="/wiki/Public_servant" class="mw-redirect" title="Public servant">public servant</a> (working at the Australian Embassy in <a href="/wiki/Jakarta,_Indonesia" class="mw-redirect" title="Jakarta, Indonesia">Jakarta, Indonesia</a> at the time of their marriage, and serving as the <a href="/wiki/Departmental_secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Departmental secretary">Secretary</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Australia)" title="Department of Foreign Affairs (Australia)">Department of Foreign Affairs</a> from 1979 to 1984), on 1 May 1955. A daughter, Roberta, named after Menzies, was born in 1956.
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Mungo_Wentworth_MacCallum" title="Mungo Wentworth MacCallum">Mungo MacCallum</a>, Menzies as prime minister engaged in an affair with Betty Fairfax, the first wife of Sir <a href="/wiki/Warwick_Oswald_Fairfax" title="Warwick Oswald Fairfax">Warwick Oswald Fairfax</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> That claim was subsequently disputed by <a href="/wiki/Gerard_Henderson" title="Gerard Henderson">Gerard Henderson</a> and Menzies's own family.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_funeral">Death and funeral</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Death and funeral">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies,_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg/220px-Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg/330px-Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg/440px-Grave_of_Robert_and_Pattie_Menzies%2C_Melbourne_General_Cemetery2017.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="3024" /></a><figcaption>Grave of Sir Robert and Dame Pattie Menzies, <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a></figcaption></figure>  
<p>Menzies died from a heart attack<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> while reading in his study at his Haverbrack Avenue home in <a href="/wiki/Malvern,_Victoria" title="Malvern, Victoria">Malvern</a>, <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a> on 15 May 1978. Tributes from across the world were sent to the Menzies family. Notably among those were from <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">HM Queen Elizabeth II</a>, <a href="/wiki/Queen_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen of Australia">Queen of Australia</a>: "I was distressed to hear of the death of Sir Robert Menzies. He was a distinguished Australian whose contribution to his country and the Commonwealth will long be remembered", and from <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Minister of Australia</a>: "All Australians will mourn his passing. Sir Robert leaves an enduring mark on Australian history."
</p><p>Menzies was accorded a <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state funeral</a>, held in <a href="/wiki/Scots%27_Church,_Melbourne" title="Scots&#39; Church, Melbourne">Scots' Church, Melbourne</a> on 19 May, at which <a href="/wiki/Charles_III_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles III of the United Kingdom">Prince Charles</a> represented <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-TheAge19780517_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TheAge19780517-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Other dignitaries to attend included current and former <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Ministers of Australia</a> <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_McEwen" title="John McEwen">John McEwen</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Gorton" title="John Gorton">John Gorton</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a> (The two surviving Labor Prime Ministers Frank Forde and Gough Whitlam did not attend the funeral), as well as the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor General of Australia">Governor General of Australia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zelman_Cowan" class="mw-redirect" title="Zelman Cowan">Sir Zelman Cowan</a>. Former <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom</a> <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Alec Douglas-Home</a> and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a> also attended.
</p><p>The service was and is to this day one of the largest state funerals ever held in Australia, with over 100,000 people lining the streets of Melbourne<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> from Scots' Church to <a href="/wiki/Springvale_Botanical_Cemetery" title="Springvale Botanical Cemetery">Springvale Crematorium</a>, where a private service was held for the Menzies family and a <a href="/wiki/21-gun_salute" title="21-gun salute">19-gun salute</a> was fired at the end of the ceremony. In July 1978, a <a href="/wiki/Burials_and_memorials_in_Westminster_Abbey" title="Burials and memorials in Westminster Abbey">memorial service</a> was held for Menzies in the United Kingdom at <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Sir Robert and Dame Pattie Menzies's ashes are interred in the 'Prime Ministers Garden' within the grounds of <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_General_Cemetery" title="Melbourne General Cemetery">Melbourne General Cemetery</a>.
</p><p>Some of Menzies's detractors also commemorated his passing in 1978, with a screenprinted poster, <i>Pig Iron Bob / Dead at last</i>, designed by <a href="/wiki/Chips_Mackinolty" title="Chips Mackinolty">Chips Mackinolty</a> from the Earthworks Poster Collective.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_views">Religious views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Religious views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Menzies was the son of a Presbyterian-turned-Methodist lay preacher and imbibed his father's Protestant faith and values. During his studies at the University of Melbourne, Menzies was president of the Students' Christian Union.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)22–23_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Allan_(1993)22–23-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Proud of his Scottish Presbyterian heritage with a living faith steeped in the Bible, Menzies nonetheless preached religious freedom and non-sectarianism as the norm for Australia. Indeed, his cooperation with Australian Catholics on the contentious state aid issue was recognised when he was invited as guest of honour to the annual Cardinal's Dinner in Sydney 1964, presided over by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Norman_Thomas_Gilroy" class="mw-redirect" title="Norman Thomas Gilroy">Norman Gilroy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoward2014325_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoward2014325-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_assessment">Legacy and assessment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Legacy and assessment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Menzies was by far the longest-serving <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Minister of Australia</a>, in office for a combined total of 18 years, five months and 12 days. His second period of 16 years, one month and seven days is by far the longest unbroken tenure in that office. During his second period he dominated Australian politics as no one else has ever done. He managed to live down the failures of his first period in office and to rebuild the conservative side of politics from the nadir it hit at the <a href="/wiki/1943_Australian_federal_election" title="1943 Australian federal election">1943 election</a>. However, it can also be noted that while retaining government on each occasion, Menzies lost the <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party-preferred vote</a> at three separate elections – in <a href="/wiki/1940_Australian_federal_election" title="1940 Australian federal election">1940</a>, <a href="/wiki/1954_Australian_federal_election" title="1954 Australian federal election">1954</a> and <a href="/wiki/1961_Australian_federal_election" title="1961 Australian federal election">1961</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was the only Australian prime minister to recommend the appointment of four <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">governors-general</a> (<a href="/wiki/William_Slim,_1st_Viscount_Slim" title="William Slim, 1st Viscount Slim">Viscount Slim</a>, and Lords <a href="/wiki/William_Morrison,_1st_Viscount_Dunrossil" title="William Morrison, 1st Viscount Dunrossil">Dunrossil</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Sidney,_1st_Viscount_De_L%27Isle" title="William Sidney, 1st Viscount De L&#39;Isle">De L'Isle</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Casey,_Baron_Casey" title="Richard Casey, Baron Casey">Casey</a>). Only two other prime ministers have ever chosen more than one governor-general.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Menzies era saw Australia become an increasingly affluent society, with average weekly earnings in 1965 50% higher in real terms than in 1945. The increased prosperity enjoyed by most Australians during this period was accompanied by a general increase in leisure time, with the five-day workweek becoming the norm by the mid-Sixties, together with three weeks of paid annual leave.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several books have been filled with anecdotes about Menzies. While he was speaking in <a href="/wiki/Williamstown,_Victoria" title="Williamstown, Victoria">Williamstown, Victoria</a>, in 1954, a heckler shouted, "I wouldn't vote for you if you were the <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Archangel Gabriel</a>"&#160;&#8211;&#32;to which Menzies coolly replied "If I were the Archangel Gabriel, I'm afraid you wouldn't be in my constituency."<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Jo_Gullett" title="Jo Gullett">Jo Gullett</a>, who first knew him as a family friend of his father, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Gullett" title="Henry Gullett">Henry Gullett</a>, wartime <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_External_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for External Affairs">Minister for External Affairs</a>, and who later served under Menzies as a Liberal Party member of parliament himself in Canberra in the 1950s, offered this assessment.
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>It is difficult to exaggerate the pre-eminence enjoyed by Menzies for over a quarter of a century in the federal Parliament. He had many natural gifts, of which the greatest lay in the quality of his mind. His capacity to absorb and retain information, facts, opinion, prejudices was most unusual. He had a particularly logical mind, so that even in his conversation, let alone his set speeches and arguments, his remarks were always in sequence. Whether he was drawing on his memory, or making up his mind as he went along, each point he made reinforced the others and added to a logical and rounded whole. He spoke like this because it was the way he thought. He would have succeeded in anything that demands a logical intellect—science, generalship, high finance, the control of great organisations ... He had a beautiful speaking voice, clear, resonant and flexible. His appearance was impressive. Finally he had most of the virtues his countrymen and women liked and respected. He was not greedy. "I have never believed in making every bob (<a href="/wiki/Shilling" title="Shilling">shilling</a>) a prisoner," he once remarked to me. So he was generous. He was a good family man, a regular churchgoer. Yet he enjoyed a party, good food and drink, loved to watch the <a href="/wiki/Test_cricket" title="Test cricket">test cricket</a> and was an <a href="/wiki/Australian_Rules_football" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Rules football">Australian Rules football</a> fan.
He shared the tastes of the people he led. At the same time none was more able than he to touch chords of loyalty and pride in the traditions and history of the British people, especially that section of them who had settled in Australia. He made us proud of ourselves. We associated him with this pride.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Planning for an official biography of Menzies began soon after his death, but it was long delayed by Dame <a href="/wiki/Pattie_Menzies" title="Pattie Menzies">Pattie Menzies</a>'s protection of her husband's reputation and her refusal to co-operate with the appointed biographer.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The reason for this is unclear. (January 2014)">why?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In 1991, the Menzies family appointed <a href="/wiki/A._W._Martin" title="A. W. Martin">A. W. Martin</a> to write a biography, which appeared in two volumes, in 1993 and 1999. In 2019, Troy Bramston, a journalist for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i> and a political historian, wrote the first biography of Menzies since Martin's two volumes, titled <i>Robert Menzies: The Art of Politics</i>. Bramston had access to previously unavailable Menzies family papers, conducted new interviews with Menzies' contemporaries and it was endorsed by Menzies' daughter, Heather Henderson. It was described as having "the most attractive combination of research and readability" of all the Menzies biographies.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/National_Museum_of_Australia" title="National Museum of Australia">National Museum of Australia</a> in Canberra holds a significant collection of memorabilia relating to Robert Menzies, including a range of medals and civil awards received by Sir Robert such as his Jubilee and Coronation medals, Order of Australia, Companion of Honour and US Legion of Merit. There are also a number of special presentation items including a walking stick, cigar boxes, silver gravy boats from the Kooyong electorate and a silver inkstand presented by Queen Elizabeth II.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Robert Menzies's personal library of almost 4,000 books is held at the University of Melbourne Library.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Published_works">Published works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Published works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>To the people of Britain at war from the Prime Minister of Australia. Speeches delivered in Great Britain in 1941. (Longmans Green and Co, 1941)</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Forgotten_People" title="The Forgotten People">The Forgotten People</a> and Other Studies in Democracy</i> (Sydney: Angus and Robertson, 1942)</li>
<li><i>Speech is of Time: Selected Speeches and Writings</i> (London: Cassell, 1958)</li>
<li><i>Afternoon Light: Some Memories of Men and Events</i> (Melbourne: Cassell Australia, 1967)</li>
<li><i>Central Power in the Australian Commonwealth: An Examination of the Growth of Commonwealth Power in the Australian Federation</i> (London: Cassell, 1967)</li>
<li><i>The Measure of the Years</i> (Melbourne: Cassell Australia, 1970)</li>
<li><i>Dark and Hurrying Days: Menzies's 1941 Diary</i> (Canberra: National Library of Australia, 1993)</li>
<li><i>Letters to My Daughter</i> (Miller's Point: Murdoch Books, 2011)</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Titles_and_honours">Titles and honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Titles and honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Spire"></span></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG/220px-Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="418" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG/330px-Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG/440px-Jeparit_Menzies_Spire.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1562" data-file-height="2970" /></a><figcaption>The Menzies Spire at <a href="/wiki/Jeparit,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeparit, Victoria">Jeparit, Victoria</a>. In part, the inscription reads: "The spire symbolises the rise to world recognition of a boy who was born in Jeparit and who rose by his own efforts to become Australia's Prime Minister and a statesman recognised and honoured throughout the world."</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li>In 1950 Menzies was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Legion_of_Merit" title="Legion of Merit">Legion of Merit</a> (Chief Commander) by US President <a href="/wiki/Harry_S._Truman" title="Harry S. Truman">Harry S. Truman</a> for "exceptionally meritorious conduct in the performance of outstanding services 1941–1944 and December 1949 – July 1950".</li>
<li>On 1 January 1951 he was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" title="Order of the Companions of Honour">Order of the Companions of Honour</a> (CH)<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>On 29 August 1952, the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a> conferred the degree of Doctor of Laws (honoris causa) on Menzies.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, He was awarded an Honorary Doctor of Laws by the Universities of <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bristol" title="University of Bristol">Bristol</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Belfast" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Belfast">Belfast</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">Melbourne</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia" title="University of British Columbia">British Columbia</a>, <a href="/wiki/McGill_University" title="McGill University">McGill</a>, <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_de_Montr%C3%A9al" title="Université de Montréal">Montreal</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Malta" title="University of Malta">Malta</a>, <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Laval</a>, <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_du_Qu%C3%A9bec" title="Université du Québec">Quebec</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Tasmania" title="University of Tasmania">Tasmania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cambridge" title="Cambridge">Cambridge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Harvard_University" title="Harvard University">Harvard</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Leeds" title="University of Leeds">Leeds</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Adelaide" title="University of Adelaide">Adelaide</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Queensland" title="University of Queensland">Queensland</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Birmingham" title="University of Birmingham">Birmingham</a>, <a href="/wiki/Drury_University" title="Drury University">Drury</a> and <a href="/wiki/University_of_California" title="University of California">California</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-hc_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hc-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1954, the <a href="/wiki/Portrait_painting" title="Portrait painting">portrait painting</a> <i>Rt. Hon. R. G. Menzies, PC, CH, KC, MP</i> by <a href="/wiki/Ivor_Hele" title="Ivor Hele">Sir Ivor Hele</a> won the <a href="/wiki/Archibald_Prize" title="Archibald Prize">Archibald Prize</a></li>
<li>On 4 April 1960, a portrait of Menzies by <a href="/wiki/William_Dargie" title="William Dargie">Sir William Dargie</a> was the front cover of <a href="/wiki/Time_magazine" class="mw-redirect" title="Time magazine">Time magazine</a>. This portrait is held by the <a href="/wiki/Art_Gallery_of_New_South_Wales" title="Art Gallery of New South Wales">Art Gallery of New South Wales</a></li>
<li>In 1963, Menzies was appointed a Knight of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Thistle" title="Order of the Thistle">Order of the Thistle</a> (KT),<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> the order being chosen in recognition of his Scottish heritage. He is the only Australian ever appointed to this order. He was the second of only two Australian prime ministers to be knighted during their term of office (the first prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a>, was knighted during his term in 1902).</li>
<li>On 29 April 1964 Menzies was awarded the honorary degree of a Doctor of Letters (DLitt) by the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Western_Australia" title="University of Western Australia">University of Western Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Menzies was also awarded with an Honorary Doctor of Science by the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-hc_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hc-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1973 Menzies was awarded Japan's <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Rising_Sun" title="Order of the Rising Sun">Order of the Rising Sun</a>, Grand Cordon, First Class (other Australian prime ministers to be awarded this honour were <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_McEwen" title="John McEwen">John McEwen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>On 7 June 1976, he was appointed a Knight of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Order of Australia</a> (AK). The category of Knight of the order had been created only on 24 May, and the Chancellor and Principal Knight of the Order, the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Kerr_(governor-general)" title="John Kerr (governor-general)">John Kerr</a>, became the first appointee, <i>ex officio</i>. Menzies's was the first appointment made after this.</li>
<li>In 1984, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Electoral_Commission" title="Australian Electoral Commission">Australian Electoral Commission</a> proclaimed at a redistribution on 14 September 1984, the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Menzies" title="Division of Menzies">Division of Menzies</a> for representation in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> in honour of the former prime minister. The division neighbours Menzies's old <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Kooyong" title="Division of Kooyong">division of Kooyong</a> in metropolitan Melbourne, Victoria.</li>
<li>In 1994, the year of the centenary of Menzies's birth, the <a href="/wiki/Menzies_Research_Centre" title="Menzies Research Centre">Menzies Research Centre</a> was created as an independent public policy think tank associated with the Liberal Party.</li>
<li>In 2009, during the <a href="/wiki/Australia_Day" title="Australia Day">Australia Day</a> celebrations, the <i>R.G. Menzies Walk</i> was officially opened by the then Governor-General, <a href="/wiki/Quentin_Bryce" title="Quentin Bryce">Quentin Bryce</a>. The walk runs alongside the northern shore of <a href="/wiki/Lake_Burley_Griffin" title="Lake Burley Griffin">Lake Burley Griffin</a> in Australia's capital, <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 2012, a life-sized bronze statue of Menzies was erected on the R.G. Menzies Walk.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Sir Robert Menzies Park, in <a href="/wiki/Wahroonga,_New_South_Wales" title="Wahroonga, New South Wales">Wahroonga, New South Wales</a><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Sir Robert Menzies Reserve, in <a href="/wiki/Malvern,_Victoria" title="Malvern, Victoria">Malvern, Victoria</a></li>
<li>The high-rise Menzies building on the <a href="/wiki/Clayton,_Victoria" title="Clayton, Victoria">Clayton</a> campus of <a href="/wiki/Monash_University" title="Monash University">Monash University</a>.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Decorations">Decorations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Decorations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png/40px-US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="11" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png/60px-US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png/80px-US_Legion_of_Merit_Chief_Commander_ribbon.png 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="123" /></a></span> 1950, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Legion_of_Merit" title="Legion of Merit">Chief Commander of the Legion of Merit</a> <span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States"><img alt="United States" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1235" data-file-height="650" /></a></span></span></li>
<li><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg/40px-Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg" decoding="async" width="40" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg/60px-Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg/80px-Lint_van_de_Orde_Compaions_of_Honour.jpg 2x" data-file-width="437" data-file-height="132" /></a></span> 1951, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" title="Order of the Companions of Honour">Companion of the Order of the Companions of Honour</a> (CH) <span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom"><img alt="United Kingdom" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="600" /></a></span></span><sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg/40px-Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="11" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg/60px-Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg/80px-Order_of_the_Thistle_UK_ribbon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="218" data-file-height="60" /></a></span> 1963, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Thistle" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Thistle">Knight of the Order of the Thistle</a> (KT) <span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland"><img alt="Scotland" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/23px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/35px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/46px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="600" /></a></span></span></li>
<li><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg/40px-JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="11" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg/60px-JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg/80px-JPN_Kyokujitsu-sho_1Class_BAR.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="218" data-file-height="60" /></a></span> 1973, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Rising_Sun" title="Order of the Rising Sun">Grand Cordon First Class of the Order of the Rising Sun</a> <span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Japan" title="Japan"><img alt="Japan" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/9e/Flag_of_Japan.svg/23px-Flag_of_Japan.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/9e/Flag_of_Japan.svg/35px-Flag_of_Japan.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/9e/Flag_of_Japan.svg/45px-Flag_of_Japan.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="600" /></a></span></span><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:OrderAustraliaRibbon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/OrderAustraliaRibbon.png/40px-OrderAustraliaRibbon.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/OrderAustraliaRibbon.png/60px-OrderAustraliaRibbon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/OrderAustraliaRibbon.png/80px-OrderAustraliaRibbon.png 2x" data-file-width="100" data-file-height="30" /></a></span> 1976, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of Australia">Knight of the Order of Australia</a> (AK) <span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Freedom_of_the_City">Freedom of the City</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Freedom of the City">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Wales" title="Wales"><img alt="Wales" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Flag_of_Wales.svg/23px-Flag_of_Wales.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Flag_of_Wales.svg/35px-Flag_of_Wales.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Flag_of_Wales.svg/46px-Flag_of_Wales.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="480" /></a></span></span> <b>29 April 1941</b>: <a href="/wiki/Swansea" title="Swansea">Swansea</a><sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland"><img alt="Scotland" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/23px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/35px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/46px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="600" /></a></span></span> <b>18 November 1948</b>: <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/England" title="England"><img alt="England" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/23px-Flag_of_England.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/35px-Flag_of_England.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/be/Flag_of_England.svg/46px-Flag_of_England.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="480" /></a></span></span> <b>6 June 1953</b>: <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a></li>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms">Arms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Arms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of Sir Robert Gordon Menzies
</caption>

<tbody><tr>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Notes</dt>
<dd>Granted by the <a href="/wiki/Lord_Lyon_King_of_Arms" title="Lord Lyon King of Arms">Lord Lyon King of Arms</a>.</dd>
<dt>Adopted</dt>
<dd>3 September 1965<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup></dd>
<dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>On a Wreath Argent and Gules, a demi-Lion Gules, its dexter paw upon a Baton Sable, about its head a chaplet of Menzies heath proper, issuant therefrom a panache of five Plumes Gules and Argent.</dd>
<dt>Helm</dt>
<dd>A Knight's helm.</dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Per chevron Argent and Azure, in base the constellation of the Southern Cross of the first, on a Chief indented Gules a seven-point Star between two Boars' heads couped Or, armed the first.</dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>Dexter, a Kangaroo proper, supporting on its sinister shoulder a banner-stave Gules, therefrom displaying a Banner of the foresaid Arms; sinister, a Lion guardant per fess indented Gules and Argent, about its head a chaplet of Menzies heath and issuant therefrom a panache of five Plumes gules and argent.</dd>
<dt>Compartment</dt>
<dd>Desert and stones proper.</dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd><a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish Gaelic language">Scottish Gaelic</a>: <i lang="gd">Vil God I Haif</i> (With the Help of God I Have)</dd>
<dt>Orders</dt>
<dd>Collar  and circlet of a Knight of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Thistle" title="Order of the Thistle">Order of the Thistle</a></dd>
<dt>Other elements</dt>
<dd><a href="/wiki/Mantling" title="Mantling">Mantling</a> Azure doubled Argent.</dd>
<dt>Symbolism</dt>
<dd>The red and white colours are taken from the <a href="/wiki/Clan_Menzies" title="Clan Menzies">Clan Menzies</a> Full Dress Tartan, as is the Menzies heath (<a href="/wiki/Rowan" title="Rowan">Rowan</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The motto is an altered version from the Clan Menzies motto ("Vil God I Zal" or "With God I Will") that replaces "Will" with "Have" to represent Menzies' achievements.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> The basis for the escutcheon is from the Menzies family coat of arms. The two gold boar heads in the escutcheon are taken from the arms of <a href="/wiki/Clan_Gordon" title="Clan Gordon">Clan Gordon</a> to represent Menzies' middle name, while the gold seven-pointed star is the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Star" title="Commonwealth Star">Commonwealth Star</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Coat of Arms of Australia">Coat of Arms of Australia</a>. The blue pile at the bottom with the white <a href="/wiki/Southern_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Southern Cross">Southern Cross</a> is taken from both the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Australia" title="Flag of Australia">Flag of Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Victoria_(state)" title="Coat of arms of Victoria (state)">the coat of arms</a> of Menzies' home state of <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(state)" title="Victoria (state)">Victoria</a>. The escutcheon is repeated in the banner held by the Kangaroo supporter, which represents Menzies' Thistle Banner which was displayed in <a href="/wiki/St_Giles%27_Cathedral" title="St Giles&#39; Cathedral">St Giles' Cathedral</a> in Edinburgh until his death. The red <a href="/wiki/Royal_Banner_of_Scotland" title="Royal Banner of Scotland">Scottish Lion</a> further represents Menzies' Scottish heritage.</dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Actors_who_have_played_Menzies">Actors who have played Menzies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Actors who have played Menzies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>In the 1984 mini series <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Bastion" title="The Last Bastion">The Last Bastion</a></i>, Menzies was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/John_Wood_(actor,_born_1946)" title="John Wood (actor, born 1946)">John Wood</a>.</li>
<li>In the 1987 mini series <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Vietnam_(TV_series)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Vietnam (TV series) (page does not exist)">Vietnam</a></i>, he was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Noel_Ferrier" title="Noel Ferrier">Noel Ferrier</a>.</li>
<li>In the 1988 mini series <i><a href="/wiki/True_Believers_(TV_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="True Believers (TV series)">True Believers</a></i>, he was portrayed by <a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Bonney_(actor)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="John Bonney (actor) (page does not exist)">John Bonney</a>.</li>
<li>In the 1996 Egyptian film <i><a href="/wiki/Nasser_56" title="Nasser 56">Nasser 56</a></i>, he was portrayed by Egyptian actor <a href="/w/index.php?title=Hassan_Kami&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Hassan Kami (page does not exist)">Hassan Kami</a>.</li>
<li>In the 2007 film <i><a href="/wiki/Curtin_(2007_film)" class="mw-redirect" title="Curtin (2007 film)">Curtin</a></i>, he was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Bille_Brown" title="Bille Brown">Bille Brown</a>.</li>
<li>In the 2008 television documentary <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Menzies_and_Churchill_at_War&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Menzies and Churchill at War (page does not exist)">Menzies and Churchill at War</a></i>, he was portrayed by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_King_(Australian_actor)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Matthew King (Australian actor) (page does not exist)">Matthew King</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Max_Gillies" title="Max Gillies">Max Gillies</a> has caricatured Menzies on stage and in the comedy satire series <i><a href="/wiki/The_Gillies_Report" title="The Gillies Report">The Gillies Report</a></i>.</li>
<li>In the 2015 documentary <i>The Dalfram Dispute 1938</i>, Menzies was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Baines" title="Bob Baines">Bob Baines</a><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 2016 Menzies was portrayed by Alan Dearth in the <i><a href="/wiki/A_Place_to_Call_Home_(TV_series)" title="A Place to Call Home (TV series)">A Place to Call Home</a></i> episode "<a href="/wiki/A_Place_to_Call_Home_(season_4)" title="A Place to Call Home (season 4)">Home to Roost</a>".</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Eponyms_of_Menzies">Eponyms of Menzies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Menzies&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Eponyms of Menzies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
